The Stanley conjecture revisited
Ryan Mickler

Abstract
. In the seminal work of Stanley [ Sta89 ] , several conjectures were made on the structure of Littlewood-Richardson coefficients for the multiplication of Jack symmetric functions. Motivated by recent results of Alexandersson and the present author [ AM23 ] , we postulate that a certain ‘windowing’ property holds for all such Jack L-R coefficients. This property provides a vast set of relations between these coefficients and allows for their direct computation in certain novel cases. We demonstrate compatibility between our windowing conjecture and the conjectures of Stanley, with the hope of illuminating the structures within.



1.
Structure of paper

In Section
2
, we begin by providing a review the titular Stanley Conjecture(s) of
[
Sta89
]
. Our first novel conjecture (
2.4
) on the general structure of Jack LR coefficients provides a modest but straightforward generalization of the strong form of Stanley’s conjecture (
2.2
). We then provide a summary of some relevant known results, and revisit the recently proven case of a rectangular union
[
AM23
]
, which was the primary motivation for this present work.

In Section
3
, we deliver a wide ranging generalization of the Rectangular Union result in the central conjecture of this paper: the Window Lensing Conjecture (
3.8
). We show that this conjecture holds in several known cases of the Stanley Conjecture (where the corresponding Schur LR coefficient is
c=1
), including the Maximal Filling Tableau case (which was proven by Stanley).
The work of Bravi-Gandini
[
BG22
]
has substantial overlap with the topics of this section, and we use their work to prove a significant case of the window lensing conjecture. We provide further evidence for this conjecture by showing that it produces a solution to a large 6-parameter family of
c=2
examples (
3.26
).

In Section
4
, we explore compatibility between the Window Lensing conjecture and a ‘factorization’ result of Bravi-Gandini
[
BG22
]
. We use these results to produce alternate formulas for the family of
c=2
examples of the previous section.

We are optimistic that the conjectures outlined in this paper, if proven, could provide a pathway to a potential proof of the Stanley Conjectures.

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Definitions

We use the same notation and conventions as
[
AM23
]
, except where stated otherwise. For a partition
\mu
of size
n
, let
J_{\mu}
denote the associated Jack symmetric function with parameter
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
, normalized so that the coefficient of the power sum variable
p_{1}^{n}
in
J_{\mu}
is
1
. These form a basis of the ring of symmetric functions. The Jack Littlewood-Richardson coefficients
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
are defined by the expansion of a product of Jack functions

(1)

J_{\mu}\cdot J_{\nu}=\sum_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}J_{\lambda}.

The Schur Littlewood-Richardson coefficients are recovered by

(2)

c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\left(\frac{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}}{}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}}{}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\nu}}{}}g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\right)_{\alpha\to 1}.

We use the following standard notation for the
\alpha
-Hall norm of the Jacks,

(3)

j_{\lambda}:=\lVert J_{\lambda}\rVert^{2}=\prod_{b\in\lambda}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\,{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}.

where the equality is given by a theorem of Stanley.

2.2.
The Stanley Conjectures

In
[
Sta89
]
, several remarkable conjectures were made regarding the
structure of the Jack Littlewood–Richardson coefficients.

Conjecture 2.1

(The Stanley Conjecture,
[
Sta89
, 8.3]
)
.

The Jack LR coefficients are ratios of non-negative integer polynomials in
\alpha
. In particular,

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\cdot j_{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\alpha].

In general, we use the terminology of a
strong
form of the Stanley conjecture to refer to any conjecture that proposes an explicit form for
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
which
manifestly
satisfies the positivity of Conjecture (
2.1
). Stanley conjectured such a form in the case
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=1
.

Conjecture 2.2

(Strong Stanley Conjecture, see
[
Sta89
, 8.5]
)
.

If
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=1
, then the corresponding Jack LR coefficient has the form

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\frac{\prod_{b\in\mu}{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\mu}(b)\,\,\prod_{b\in\nu}{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\nu}(b)}{\prod_{b\in\lambda}{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\lambda}(b)},

where
\Phi_{b}
is a choice of either
U
or
L
for each box
b
in
\mu,\nu
and
\lambda
.
Moreover, one chooses
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\,}(b)}
and
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\,}(b)}
exactly the same number of times in the numerator and denominator.

2.3.
Structural Conjecture

In the closing section of
[
Sta89
]
, Stanley contemplates whether an expression of the type of Conjecture (
2.2
) may also hold for cases where
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\geq 1
, where one might instead sum over
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}
terms of the form given therein. However, a counter example was presented that halted this idea (see Example
4.15
). In this paper we pursue that train of thought, and we will build evidence for the following straightforward generalization.

Definition 2.3
.

For any collections of boxes
\mu,\nu,\lambda
(e.g. partitions), define the set of
channels

\mathcal{H}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
as the set of assignments
\Phi:b\mapsto\{U,L\}
for every box
b
in each of
\mu,\nu
or
\lambda
.

For a hook assignment
A\in\{U,L\}
, we let
\bar{A}
denote the opposite assignment.

Conjecture 2.4

(General Structure)
.

For each
\mu,\nu,\lambda
, there exist a choice of
channel coefficients

c_{\Phi}\in\mathbb{Z}
for
\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
such that

(4)

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}}c_{\Phi}\cdot\frac{\prod_{b\in\mu}{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\mu}(b)\,\,\prod_{b\in\nu}{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\nu}(b)}{\prod_{b\in\lambda}{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\lambda}(b)}.

Consequentially, we have

(5)

c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}}c_{\Phi}.

We observe that Conjecture (
2.4
) is compatible with the Stanley Conjecture (
2.1
) in that the poles of
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\alpha)
as a meromorphic function of
\alpha
can only be of the form
1/{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}/L}}_{\lambda}(b)}
. However, Conjecture (
2.4
) is much weaker in the sense that it does not suggest any positivity property of the coefficients
c_{\Phi}
. We do however make a modest refinement to this positivity conjecture later in (
3.17
).

Above, we introduced the notion of
channels
, which capture the
assignment
of an upper or lower hook to a particular box in a diagram, which is more general than considering only the hook
lengths
corresponding to such an assignment. We now formalize this distinction.

For a box
b
in the tableau associated to a partition
\lambda
, we will consider the assignment of an upper or lower hook to be denoted
1
1

1

In
[
AM23
]
, this notation is used instead to denote the hook
vector
, a closely related concept.
by the hook
symbols
of either
{\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}
or
{\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}
, and the hook
lengths
(i.e. the
\alpha
-content of that hook) to be denoted
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}
or
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}
. We use bullet
(\bullet)
to indicate ambiguity between
U
or
L
, e.g.
\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\lambda}(b)
. Inside a tableau, we denote upper hook symbol by

U

and lower hook symbol by

L

. For example, for the partition
\lambda=532
and the box
b=(1,0)
,

(6)

The upper hook
symbol
is

(7)

{\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower Definition 2.5
.

To such a choice, we associate a fraction of products of hook symbols, known as a
channel factor

(8)

\bm{G}_{\Phi}\coloneqq\frac{\prod_{b\in\mu}{\bm{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{{\mu}}(b)}\,\,\prod_{b\in\nu}{\bm{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\nu}(b)}}{\prod_{b\in\lambda}{\bm{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{\lambda}(b)}}.

In such a factor, boxes for
\lambda
appear in the denominator, and boxes for
\mu,\nu
appear in the numerator. For example for
\lambda=32

\mu=21
, and
\nu=1

(9)

A
rule
is a choice of an element in the
\mathbb{Z}
span of such elements, denoted
\mathcal{H}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}(\mathbb{Z})
. The evaluation map
[.]
extends to a map
\mathcal{H}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathbb{Q}(\alpha)
.

With this definition we may restate the General Structure Conjecture (
2.4
) as

Conjecture 2.6

(General Structure)
.

There exists a rule
{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
that describes Jack Littlewood-Richardson coefficients. That is, there exists

(10)

{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}}c_{\Phi}\,\bm{G}_{\Phi}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(\mathbb{Z})\quad\text{with }c_{\Phi}\in\mathbb{Z},

with the property that
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=[{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}]
.

Where appropriate in the remainder of this paper, we freely move between considering
{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
and its evaluation.

2.4.
Known Cases

Here we collect some known cases of the Strong Stanley conjecture (
2.2
), that is, in all the cases below the corresponding Schur LR coefficient satisfies
c=1
.

Theorem 2.7

(Pieri rule,
[
Sta89
, 6.1]
)
.

Let
\lambda/\mu
be a horizontal
r
-strip, then the Jack LR coefficient is given by the following expression

g_{\mu,(r)}^{\lambda}=\frac{\prod_{b\in\mu}A_{\mu,\lambda/\mu}(b)\,\,\prod_{b\in(r)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{(r)}(b)}}{\prod_{b\in\lambda}A_{\lambda,\lambda/\mu}(b)},

where

A_{\sigma,\lambda/\mu}(b)\coloneqq\begin{cases}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\sigma}(b)}&\text{ if $\lambda/\mu$ does not contain a box in the same column as $b$}\\
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Example 2.8
.

{\bm{g}}_{75411,3}^{76431}=\frac{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxL}\vbox to11.65326pt{\vss\hbox to11.24445pt{\hss$L$\hss}\vss}}\kern-11.65326pt\vrule height=11.65326pt,width=0.40881pt\kern Stanley also generalized the above Pieri rule to the case of Maximal Filling tableaus.

Theorem 2.9

(Maximal Filling Case
[
Sta89
, 8.6]
)
.

Suppose that
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=1
, and the unique
LR
-tableau
T
of shape
\lambda/\mu
with weight
\nu
has the
maximal filling
property, that is, that every column
C
of
T
consists of the sequential integers
1,2,\ldots,n_{C}
.
Let
r_{i}
be defined as the largest entry of
T
in row
i
of
\lambda/\mu
, and let
c_{j}
be the largest entry of
T
in column
j
of
\lambda/\mu
. For
b=(j,i)
define

(11)

C_{\sigma,T}(b)\coloneqq\begin{cases}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}&\text{ if $r_{i}\leq c_{j}$ and $c_{j}>0$}\\
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\sigma}(b)}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Furthermore, for
b=(j,i)
, let
b_{*}\coloneqq(j,i+c_{j})
. The Jack LR coefficient is given by

(12)

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\frac{\prod_{b\in\mu}C_{\mu,T}(b_{*})\cdot\prod_{b\in\nu}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\nu}(b)}}{\prod_{b\in\lambda}C_{\lambda,T}(b)}.

Example 2.10

(
[
Sta89
, p113-114]
)
.

Take
\lambda=766654211
,
\mu=7553322
,
\nu=542
. The unique
LR
tableau
T
of shape
\lambda/\mu
and weight
\nu
is given by

We see that
T
has the maximal filling property, with vectors
c=\{2,0,1,3,2,3,0\}
, and
r=\{0,1,2,3,2,3,0,1,2\}
. Using the formula above, we find the following expression for the rule

Theorem 2.11

(Rectangular case, see
[
CJ13
, 4.7]
)
.

Let
\mu\subseteq m^{n}
, and
\bar{\mu}
be the partition that is the reverse
3
3

3

I.e. rotate the shape 180 degrees.
of the shape
m^{n}/\mu
. Then the strong Stanley conjecture (
2.2
) holds for
g_{\mu,\bar{\mu}}^{m^{n}}
, in particular

(13)

g_{\mu,\bar{\mu}}^{m^{n}}=\frac{\prod_{b\in\mu}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}(b)}\,\,\prod_{b\in\bar{\mu}}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\bar{\mu}}(b)}}{\prod_{b\in m^{n}}D^{\prime}_{\mu,m^{n}}(b)},

where, for
b=(b_{1},b_{2})\in m^{n}
, we define

(14)

D^{\prime}_{\mu,m^{n}}(b)\coloneqq\begin{cases}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{m^{n}}(b)}&\text{ if $(b_{1},n-1-b_{2})\in\mu$}\\
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{m^{n}}(b)}&\text{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

11.24445pt\vrule height=11.65326pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-11.65326pt\hrule width=12.06207pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 11.24445pt\hrule width=12.06207pt,height=0.40881pt}&\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxU}\vbox to11.65326pt{\vss\hbox to11.24445pt{\hss$U$\hss}\vss}}\kern-11.65326pt\vrule height=11.65326pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 11.24445pt\vrule height=11.65326pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-11.65326pt\hrule width=12.06207pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 11.24445pt\hrule width=12.06207pt,height=0.40881pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}}.

Note: as with all Jack LR cofficients
g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
the evaluations
g_{\mu,\bar{\mu}}^{m^{n}}=g_{\bar{\mu},\mu}^{m^{n}}
are symmetric in the lower arguments
4
4

4

For a demonstration of this property in this case see
[
AM23
]
, however in this case the corresponding
rules

{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\bar{\mu}}^{m^{n}}
are not. A priori, we don’t have any reason to expect that rules need be symmetric in these arguments, in fact, the evidence herein suggests them not to be.

The next example is the motivation for the present work. Wherein the surprising simplicity of the answer, and the compatibility with the Rectangular case (
2.11
), merited further investigation.

Theorem 2.13

(Rectangular union case,
[
AM23
]
Corollary 3.6 )
.

Let
m^{n}
be a rectangular partition, and
\mu
be any partition (i.e. not necessarily
m^{n}\subset\mu
). Let
\sigma\coloneqq m^{n}\cap\mu
, and
\bar{\sigma}
be the partition given by the reverse of
m^{n}/\sigma
. Then, the Strong Stanley Conjecture (
2.2
) holds for
g_{\mu,\bar{\sigma}}^{\mu\cup m^{n}}
.

In particular, let
R\subset m^{n}
be the smallest rectangular shape such that
\mu
and
\mu\cup m^{n}
agree outside
R
. Say
R
is of shape
k^{\ell}
. Let
\mu_{R}
be the partition giving by shifting the shape
\mu\cap R
back to the origin, and
\bar{\mu}_{R}
be the reverse of
k^{\ell}/\mu_{R}
. We then have the following decomposition for the LR coefficient

(15)

g_{\mu,\bar{\sigma}}^{\mu\cup m^{n}}=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[scale,page]{stanleyConjecturePics}}\times g_{\mu_{R},\bar{\mu}_{R}}^{k^{\ell}}

where the left factor (henceforth denoted
F_{R}
) is a products of
L
-hooks for every box to the left of
R
(denoted by dashed line),
and
U
-hooks for every box below it, for both
\mu
in the numerator
and
\mu\cup m^{n}
in the denominator. For boxes sharing neither a row or column with a box in
R
, we can assign
either
upper or lower hooks as these cancel between the numerator and denominator, and so we indicate this ambiguity with light grey

. The second factor
g_{\mu_{R},\bar{\mu}_{R}}^{k^{\ell}}
is covered by the rectangular case above.

Thus we see that the rule for the Rectangular union case,
{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\bar{\sigma}}^{\mu\cup m^{n}}
, is equivalent to starting with the rule for the Rectangular case (
2.11
) inside the ‘window’
R
, given by
{\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R},\bar{\mu}_{R}}^{k^{\ell}}
, and extending outside of this window by
F_{R}
.

The notation
({\bm{g}})_{R}
denotes taking hook choices for the boxes inside
R\cap\mu
,
R\cap\lambda
and
\nu
to agree with those choices of the rule
{\bm{g}}
inside the brackets.

3.
Windowing Conjecture

You rows of houses! you window-pierc’d façades! you roofs!

You porches and entrances! you copings and iron guards!

You windows whose transparent shells might expose so much!

You doors and ascending steps! you arches!

You gray stones of interminable pavements! you trodden crossings!

From all that has touch’d you I believe you have imparted to yourselves, and now would impart the same secretly to me…

Walt Whitman

Song of the Open Road

Motivated by the Rectangular Union case (
2.13
), we consider the following constructions.

Definition 3.1
.

A
window
is a shape
R
that is closed under meets and joins of its boxes (hence, necessarily a union of rectangles).

For a partition
\mu
, let
\mu_{R}
be the
windowed partition
obtained by viewing
\mu
through the window
R
. That is,
\mu_{R}
is given by the shape
\mu\cap R
joined along edges of
R
and re-centered back to the origin as a partition. We color the boxes of windows with beige

.

For any shape
\sigma
, define the
meet-join
of
\sigma
, denoted
{\Diamond}(\sigma)
, to be the window that is given by the closure of
\sigma
under meets and joins of its boxes. In particular, for
\mu\subset\lambda
we often consider
{\Diamond}(\lambda/\mu)
, which is the smallest window such that
\mu
and
\lambda
agree outside of it.

Example 3.2
.

For
\lambda=44322
, and the window
R
given below, we have
\lambda_{R}=3211
.

For further demonstration, let
\mu\subset\lambda
be such that
\mu
and
\lambda
agree (as shapes) outside of two disjoint
5
5

5

And which can be totally separated horizontally and vertically.
rectangles
R_{1},R_{2}
. Let
R_{3}={R_{1}\mathbin{\curlywedge}R_{2}}
and
R_{4}={R_{1}\mathbin{\curlyvee}R_{2}}
.

(16)

Viewing through the window
R\coloneqq R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}+R_{4}
, the shapes are joined on their edges, defining the two windowed partitions
\lambda_{R}
and
\mu_{R}
.

Following from Formula (
15
), we make the following key definitions.

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mu\subset\lambda
be such that
\mu
and
\lambda
agree (as shapes) outside of a window
R
, i.e.
{\Diamond}({\lambda/\mu})\subset R
.

Define the
window factor
for
R
, denoted
\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}
, as the ratio of hook symbols given by the following product over all boxes in
\mu
and
\lambda
which are outside
R
,

(17)

\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}\coloneqq\prod_{b\in\mu/R}\frac{{{\bm{h}}^{\bullet}_{\mu}(b)}}{{{\bm{h}}^{\bullet}_{\lambda}(b)}},

where for those boxes that:

•

share a row with a box in
R
, we assign a lower hook symbol

.

•

share a column with a box in
R
, we assign a upper hook symbol

.

•

share neither a row or column with a box in
R
, then in this case the hook lengths for this box in
\mu
and
\lambda
agree, and thus they will cancel out when the hook symbols are evaluated. Thus, we can assign either an upper or lower hook symbol (or neither), an ambiguity indicated by the neutral symbol

.

For example, if we consider the window given by Figure (
16
), we have

(18)

\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}:=\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[scale,page]{stanleyConjecturePics}}

Correspondingly, let
F_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}\coloneqq[\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}]
be the evaluation that sends hook symbols to hook lengths. Often we just write
\bm{F}_{R}\equiv\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}
if the context of the partitions is clear.

Example 3.4
.

In the earlier example (
2.14
), with
\lambda=43331
and
\mu=42211
, we have

Lemma 3.5
.

If
\lambda/\mu\subseteq R^{\prime}\subset R
, then
\bm{F}_{\mu,R^{\prime}}^{\lambda}
agrees with
\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}
outside of
R
. Thus any window containing
\lambda/\mu
is compatible with the smallest such window
{\Diamond}(\lambda/\mu)
.

Proof.

Any box
b
outside of
R
that lies in a row (or column) that intersects
R^{\prime}
but not
R
has the property that
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}/L}}_{\mu}(b)}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}/L}}_{\lambda}(b)}
, and hence there is a cancellation between this hook length in the numerator and denominator, and thus we assign this box the neutral symbol.
∎

Definition 3.6
.

For a window
R
, define the
de-windowing
operator
\left(\cdot\right)_{R}
by its action on hook symbols as

(19)

\left(\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\lambda_{R}}(b)\right)_{R}\coloneqq\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\lambda}(b^{R}),

where the box
b\in\lambda_{R}
corresponds to
b^{R}\in\lambda\cap R
. This operator is extended by linearity to act on rules.

For ease of notation, we write de-windowing followed by evaluation as

[\cdot]_{R}\coloneqq[\,(\cdot)_{R}\,].

Example 3.7
.

In the case of Example (
3.2
), we have

\left(\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 3.1.
Main Conjecture

Motivated by the Rectangular Union case (
2.13
), we propose that a similar formula of that type holds in general for all Jack LR coefficients. The following is the main conjecture of this paper.

Conjecture 3.8

(Window Lensing)
.

Assume the General Structure Conjecture (
2.6
) holds. The Jack Littlewood-Richardson rules
{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
satisfy the
window lensing
property: for a window
R
with
\lambda/\mu\subset R
, we have

(20)

{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}={\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}}\times\left({\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}\right)_{R}.

According to this conjecture, to prescribe a rule for a given Jack LR coefficient which is constrained by a window
R
, we fill in the boxes outside of
R
with the window factor
\bm{F}_{R}
, and inside
R
we fill in with the de-windowed rule
{\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}
(extended by linearity over the sum of terms in the rule). We can window over the pair
\mu,\lambda
, or
\nu,\lambda
, and such a formula should hold for each pair. As before, we don’t assume the rule
{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
is symmetric in the two lower arguments.

This conjecture gives a vast set of relations between the various LR coefficients, which we investigate in the next subsection.

3.2.
Consequences

In this section, we provide evidence for the Window Lensing conjecture, by considering special cases of it.

Firstly, we consider the case where
\mu\subset\lambda
are such that
\mu
and
\lambda
agree outside of a window
R
that is a single rectangle. In this case, we have
[{\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}]_{R}=g_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}
, and the window lensing conjecture reduces to the following result, which we are able to prove as a straightforward consequence of the work of Bravi-Gandini
[
BG22
]
.

Theorem 3.9

(Locality)
.

Let
\mu\subset\lambda
be such that
\lambda/\mu
in contained inside a rectangular window
R
. We then have

(21)

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=F_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}\times g_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}},

where, as before,

\bm{F}_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}\coloneqq\raisebox{-0.5pt}{\includegraphics[scale,page]{stanleyConjecturePics}}

Proof.

We show this follows directly from a result of Bravi-Gandini
[
BG22
, Theorem 10]
. For
\lambda\subset\mu
, let
J_{\lambda/\mu}
denote the skew Jack function. For
b\in\mu
, define
6
6

6

In
[
BG22
]
, the notation
c_{\mu,\lambda,s}\equiv h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(s)
, and
c^{\prime}_{\lambda,\mu,s}\equiv h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(s)
is used.

(22)

h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)=\arm_{\mu}(b)\alpha+\leg_{\lambda}(b)+1,\,\,\,h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)\coloneqq(\arm_{\lambda}(b)+1)\alpha+\leg_{\mu}(b).

For the window
R
consisting of a single rectangle, let
\lambda_{R}
and
\mu_{R}
be the corresponding windowed partitions.
In the language of Bravi-Gandini, the skew shapes
\lambda/\mu
and
\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}

coincide up to translation
, in which case they show the following relation of skew Jack functions

(23)

h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}\cdot J_{\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}}=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu_{R},\lambda_{R}}h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda_{R},\mu_{R}}\cdot J_{\lambda/\mu},

where
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}\coloneqq\prod_{b\in\mu}h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)
, and
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}\coloneqq\prod_{b\in\mu}h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)
.
We notice that for a box
b\in\mu\cap R\subseteq\mu
, we have
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda_{R},\mu_{R}}(b_{R})
. Thus, after cancelling factors from all boxes inside
R
, we re-write equation (
23
) as

(24)

J_{\lambda/\mu}=\prod_{b\in\mu/R}h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)\cdot J_{\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}}.

As we are interested in LR coefficients, which are given by
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\langle J_{\lambda/\mu}/j_{\lambda},J_{\nu}\rangle
, we write the above in terms of the quantities
J_{\lambda/\mu}/j_{\lambda}
,

(25)

\frac{J_{\lambda/\mu}}{j_{\lambda}}=\prod_{b\in\mu/R}\frac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\cdot\frac{J_{\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}}}{j_{\lambda_{R}}}.

Now, if
b\in\mu/R
does not share a row or column with
R
, then
\arm_{\mu}(b)=\arm_{\lambda}(b)
and
\leg_{\mu}(b)=\leg_{\lambda}(b)
, and hence
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)
, and similarly
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu}(b)
. We see that the contribution from those such boxes
b
to the product in (
25
) vanishes. Thus, that same product reduces to one over the two regions
R_{below}
and
R_{left}
, of those boxes
b\in\mu/R
either below or left of
R
. For a box
b\in R_{left}
, we have
\leg_{\mu}(b)=\leg_{\lambda}(b)
, and thus
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)
, and
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda,\mu}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)
. For a box
b\in R_{below}
, we have
\arm_{\mu}(b)=\arm_{\lambda}(b)
, and thus
h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu,\lambda}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)
and
h^{U}_{\lambda/\mu}(b)=h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu}(b)
. Thus we have shown that equation (
25
) reduces to

(26)

\frac{J_{\lambda/\mu}}{j_{\lambda}}=\prod_{b\in R_{left}}\frac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\prod_{b\in R_{below}}\frac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\cdot\frac{J_{\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}}}{j_{\lambda_{R}}}.

We see this factor is precisely
F^{\lambda}_{\mu,R}
, that is,

(27)

\frac{J_{\lambda/\mu}}{j_{\lambda}}=F^{\lambda}_{\mu,R}\cdot\frac{J_{\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}}}{j_{\lambda_{R}}}.

Taking the inner product with
J_{\nu}
, we recover the result.
∎

Corollary 3.10
.

The Rectangular union case (
2.13
) follows immediately from the Locality property (
3.9
).

In
[
AM23
]
, the Rectangular Union LR coefficient was calculated directly using a very different approach. The corollary above demonstrates a vast simplification over that calculation.

.

The next result demonstrates the compatibility between the Strong Stanley conjecture (
2.2
) and the Window Lensing conjecture (
3.8
). First we note compatibillity between Schur LR coefficients and windowing.

Lemma 3.12
.

For any
\mu\subset\lambda
, and
R
a window with
\lambda/\mu\subseteq R
, and for all
\nu
, we have the equality of Schur Littlewood-Richardson coeffiecients

(28)

c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=c_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}.

Proof.

Follows from a simple counting of LR tableau, or from the equality of skew Schur functions
s_{\lambda/\mu}=s_{\lambda_{R}/\mu_{R}}
.
∎

Corollary 3.13

(Simple Lensing)
.

Assume
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=1
. Let
\lambda/\mu\subset R
as before. As noted in Lemma (
3.12
), we also have
c_{\mu_{R}\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}=1
inside the window
R
.
Assuming the Strong Stanley conjecture (
2.2
), there exists a one-term rule
{\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}
. Then, by assuming the Window Lensing property (
3.8
), a one-term rule for
{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}
is given by

{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=\bm{F}_{R}\times\left({\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R},\nu}^{\lambda_{R}}\right)_{R}.

Example 3.14
.

We show that the Pieri rule (Theorem (
2.7
)) is a special case of the Window Lensing conjecture (
3.8
), in the form of the Simple Lensing property (
3.13
). We consider the earlier example ((
2.8
)), however now we note that we can cancel several hooks in the fraction that appears within, to recover a simpler picture:

Example 3.15
.

Consider the earlier Maximal Filling tableau example ((
2.10
)) of
\lambda=766654211
,
\mu=7553322
,
\nu=542
. We will show this is also an example of the Window Lensing conjecture (
3.8
). As in the previous example, we cancel out hooks that appear equally in the numerator and denominator and extract the minimal window
R={\Diamond}(\lambda/\mu)

Indeed, we can show the example of (
3.15
) holds in general.

Theorem 3.16
.

The Window Lensing conjecture (
3.8
) holds in the Maximal Filling Tableau case.

Proof.

Firstly, we show that outside
R={\Diamond}(\lambda/\mu)
the assignment rule (
12
) yields a factor of
\bm{F}_{R}
. We note that
R
can be described as the union of all columns satisfying
c_{j}=0
with all rows satisfying
r_{i}=0
. For each box in a column with
c_{j}=0
, the rule (
11
) assigns all lower hooks. Of the boxes
b
in such columns that are in rows with
r_{i}=0
have
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}(b_{*})}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}(b)}
since for such boxes we have
b_{*}=b
. Since the partitions are identical in that row-column, these hooks factors cancel between the numerator and denominator. Thus each such column agrees with
\bm{F}_{R}
. For the remaining hook choices outside
R
, that is, those in rows with
r_{i}=0
and in positions with
c_{j}>0
, we can just look at the transposed problem (as it it known that this prescription of hooks is invariant under transposition of all data), the solution to which is given by flipping all hooks, and thus the previous argument for columns with
c_{j}=0
must apply now to rows with
r_{i}=0
, however with upper hooks instead of lower hooks. Thus, outside of R we assign hooks according to
\bm{F}_{R}
.

Next, we need to show that the assignment of hooks inside
R
agrees with the same rule applied to
\lambda_{R},\mu_{R}
. First we note that the maximal filling LR tableau agrees with its windowed version
T_{R}
. Hence the vectors of windowed row-column maximums
c^{R},r^{R}
, for the windowed LR tableau
T_{R}
agree with the un-windowed
c,r
however will all zeros removed. Thus, for each box
b^{\prime}\coloneqq(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})
in the windowed partitions
\lambda_{R}
, corresponding to the unwindowed box
b=(j,i)
in
R
, we have the equivalent conditions
c^{R}_{j^{\prime}}\leq r_{i^{\prime}}^{R}\Leftrightarrow c_{j}\leq r_{i}
. Hence the windowed choice of hooks inside
\lambda_{R}
agrees with the un-windowed choices inside
R\cap\lambda
, i.e.
d_{\lambda_{R},T_{R}}(b^{\prime})=d_{\lambda,T}(b)
, where
d_{\lambda,T}\in\{U,L\}
represents the
choice
of hook for
C_{\lambda,T}
in formula (
11
), and
D_{\mu,T}(b)\coloneqq d_{\lambda,T}(b_{*})
be the corresponding choices for
\mu
.

So, to complete the proof we have to show that the hook choices all boxes
b^{\prime}=(j^{\prime},i^{\prime})\in\mu_{R}
also agree with those for their un-windowed counterparts
b=(j,i)\in\mu
, that is, we need to show that

(31)

D_{\mu_{R},T_{R}}(b^{\prime})=D_{\mu,T}(b),

or equivalently
d_{\lambda_{R},T_{R}}((b^{\prime})_{*})=d_{\lambda,T}(b_{*})
, where as before
b_{*}\coloneqq(j,i+c_{j})
.

The complication is that we may have removed certain rows from
\mu
that have
r_{i}=0
(i.e, those in
R
), and so we have to check that these hook choices still agree after taking into consideration the vertical shift by
c_{j}
. In other words, it is
not
the case that
(b^{\prime})_{*}=(b_{*})^{\prime}
for all
b
. If indeed this was so, equation (
31
) would follow immediately, since then we would have
d_{\lambda_{R},T_{R}}((b^{\prime})_{*})=d_{\lambda_{R},T_{R}}((b_{*})^{\prime})=d_{\lambda,T}(b_{*})
. This complication is demonstrated in the following example of
\mu=44441,\nu=431,\lambda=5554321
.

T:=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$1$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$2$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule 690.4pt}}$}}.\\

In this example, we find the following box correspondences,

(a^{\prime})_{*}=(a_{*})^{\prime},\quad f=(b_{*})^{\prime}=(c^{\prime})^{*},\quad g=(c_{*})^{\prime}=(d^{\prime})_{*}\quad(e^{\prime})_{*}=(e_{*})^{\prime}.

In
\mu
the row containing
b
is removed, in
\lambda
the row containing
d_{*}
is removed. Here we see that the two boxes
c,d
in
\mu
, ie, those that are in rows at up to
c_{j}
below the row that is being removed (the one containing
b
), need to be shifted up by 1 row inside
\lambda
for the correspondence to work out.

We will show that indeed the hook choices are consistent when one removes only a single row, and then by iteration the result will hold for all
R
. So let
R
be the complement of a single row,
\tau
. All the considerations below are for boxes in a column with
c_{j}>0
.

We have shown that for boxes
x\in\mu
at most
c_{j}
rows beneath the row
\tau
in
\mu
, which we say are
close
to
\tau
, we have

(x_{*}+(0,1))^{\prime}=(x^{\prime})_{*},

whereas for all other boxes one can easily show
(x_{*})^{\prime}=(x^{\prime})_{*}
.
We have to check now that the rule (
11
) assigns consistent hooks for
x
close to
\tau
across the four boxes
x,x^{\prime},(x^{\prime})_{*}
and
x_{*}+(0,1)
.

For any
x
that is between
1
and
c(x)
rows below
\tau
in
\mu
,
x_{*}=x+(0,c(x))
is at most
c(x)-1
rows
above
the row
\tau
in
\lambda
. Then let
y_{*}=x_{*}+(0,1)
be the box that satisfies
(y_{*})^{\prime}=(x^{\prime})_{*}
. We then know that
y_{*}
is at most
c(x)
rows above the row
\tau
in
\lambda
.

Next, consider
z_{*}\in\lambda
in the row
\tau
in
\lambda
, for which we have
c(z_{*})>0
and
r(z_{*})=0
. We will show that
d_{\lambda,T}(z_{*})=U=d_{\lambda,T}(z_{*}+(0,k))
for all
k\leq c(z_{*})
. In particular this applies to
y_{*}
from the previous paragraph. We recall that the maximal filling condition forces the the entries of the LR tableau to be increasing incrementally as we move up columns, and so because we know that
r(z_{*})=0
we must have
r(z_{*}+(0,k))\leq k
. Thus, for
k\leq c(z_{*})
, we find
r(z_{*}+(0,k))\leq c(z_{*})=c(z_{*}+(0,k))>0
, and so
d_{\lambda,T}(z_{*}+(0,k))=U
.

Thus for
x
close to
\tau
, we have shown the following agreement of hook assignments:

\displaystyle D_{\mu,T}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\lambda,T}(x_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\lambda,T}(x_{*}+(0,1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\lambda_{R},T_{R}}((y_{*})^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle d_{\lambda_{R},T_{R}}((x^{\prime})_{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{\mu_{R},T_{R}}(x^{\prime})

For
x
not close to
\tau
, we have
(x^{\prime})_{*}=(x_{*})^{\prime}
and so the same agreement follows directly. Thus the theorem is proven.
∎

3.3.
Positivity Conjecture

With the constructions of the previous sections, we can propose a sharpening of the Stanley Conjecture (
2.1
).

Conjecture 3.17

(Window Positivity)
.

Let
\mu\subset\lambda
be such that
\lambda/\nu\subset R
, for a window
R
. In terms of the positivity of Conjecture (
2.1
), the factor
F_{R}
accounts for all boxes outside
R
in the following precise sense:

(32)

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\cdot\left(F_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}\right)^{-1}\cdot j_{\lambda,R}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\alpha],

where
j_{\lambda,R}\coloneqq\prod_{b\in\lambda\cap R}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}
.

Note that
F_{R}
is a rational function in
\alpha
with numerator and denominator each a positive polynomial of equal degrees, and
j_{\lambda,R}
is a positive polynomial of degree
2|\lambda\cap R|
, and so conjecture (
3.17
) claims a stronger positivity than that of Conjecture (
2.1
).

Example 3.18
.

To demonstrate conjecture (
3.17
), we take
\mu=654321
,
\nu=211
,
\lambda=664432
. We find

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=\frac{\alpha^{2}(2+3\alpha)f(\alpha)}{2700(1+\alpha)^{12}(3+2\alpha)(4+3\alpha)}

where
f(\alpha)
is an irreducible degree 8 positive polynomial.
We take the minimal window
R
,

height=7.56429pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-7.56429pt\hrule width=7.9731pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 7.15547pt\hrule width=7.9731pt,height=0.40881pt}\cr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}}},\quad F_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}=\frac{{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!0}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{5,\!4}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{0,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{4,\!5}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!4}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!0}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{0,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}}{{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{5,\!5}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{5,\!5}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{4,\!4}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}}

where
{{\lfloor}\!{a,\!b}\!{\rfloor}\!}\coloneqq a+b\alpha
. We also have

j_{\lambda,R}=(\!{{\lfloor}\!{0,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!0}\!{\rfloor}\!})^{4}(\!{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!})^{2}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{4,\!5}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{5,\!4}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!4}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{4,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}.

We check the window positivity conjecture

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\cdot\left(F_{\mu,R}^{\lambda}\right)^{-1}\cdot j_{\lambda,R}=4\alpha^{4}(2+\alpha)(1+2\alpha)(2+3\alpha)f(\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\alpha].

Note that the Stanley conjecture (
2.1
) gives the weaker positivity property

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\cdot j_{\lambda}=(\text{ degree 32 polynomial in }\alpha\,)f(\alpha)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}[\alpha].

Example 3.19

(P. Hanlon’s example
[
Sta89
, p114]
)
.

Take
\mu=31
,
\nu=21
,
\lambda=421
. Here we have
c=2
, and the LR coefficient is given by

g_{31,21}^{421}=\frac{\alpha(9+97\alpha+294\alpha^{2}+321\alpha^{3}+131\alpha^{4}+12\alpha^{5})}{3(1+\alpha)^{3}(2+\alpha)(1+2\alpha)^{2}(1+3\alpha)}

We take the minimal
R={\Diamond}(\lambda/\mu)
, which gives

To verify conjecture (
3.17
), we check

g_{31,21}^{421}\cdot F_{R}^{-1}\cdot j_{\lambda,R}=4\alpha^{4}(9+97\alpha+294\alpha^{2}+321\alpha^{3}+131\alpha^{4}+12\alpha^{5}),

with

\displaystyle j_{\lambda,R}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{{\lfloor}\!{0,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}^{3}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!2}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{2,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{3,\!3}\!{\rfloor}\!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 12\,\alpha^{3}(1+2\alpha)^{2}(2+\alpha)(1+3\alpha)(1+\alpha)^{2}.

3.4.
Further Consequences

In this section, we demonstrate more examples that provide evidence for the Window Lensing conjecture (
3.8
).

As expected, this answer agrees with the known result from the Pieri rule (
2.7
).

Example 3.21
.

Let
\mu=(1+N)1^{M}
,
\nu=(1+s)1^{p}
,
\lambda=(1+N+s)1^{M+p+1}
. Here we have
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=1
.

We have
\mu_{R_{1}}=(1)
,
\lambda_{R_{1}}=(1+s)1^{p+1}
, and Window Lensing says that

(40)

{\bm{g}}_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=\bm{F}_{R_{1}}\left({\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R_{1}},\nu}^{\lambda_{R_{1}}}\right)_{R_{1}}.

Now for the inner argument,
{\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R_{1}},\nu}^{\lambda_{R_{1}}}
, we use the results from Example (
3.20
), and window on the arguments
\nu,\lambda_{R_{1}}
,

(41)

{\bm{g}}_{(1+N)1^{M},(1+s)1^{p}}^{(1+N+s)1^{M+p+1}}=\frac{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower This answer agrees with known results.

In this previous example, we see the composition of multiple window lensing

(42)

{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\bm{F}_{R_{1}}\left(\bm{F}_{R_{2}}\left({\bm{g}}_{\mu_{R_{1}},\nu_{R_{2}}}^{\lambda_{R_{1}\circ R_{2}}}\right)_{R_{2}}\right)_{R_{1}}.

Definition 3.22
.

When composing multiple windows
R=R_{1}\circ R_{2}
, we define the
composite window factor

(43)

\bm{F}_{R}\coloneqq\bm{F}_{R_{1}}\left(\bm{F}_{R_{2}}\right)_{R_{1}}.

We similarly define a composite de-windowing operation

({\bm{g}})_{R}:=\left(({\bm{g}})_{R_{2}}\right)_{R_{1}}.

Example 3.23
.

The examples (
3.20
) and (
3.21
) combine together to give the composite de-windowing

(44)

These previous examples are a warm up for the following non-trivial consequence of the Window Lensing conjecture. This example will be the focus of the rest of this article.

Example 3.24
.

Consider the 6 parameter
(K,M,N,T,p,s)
family of problems given by
\lambda=(2+K+N+s+1)(K+2)^{M+1}(1)^{T+p+1}
,
\mu=(K+2+N)(K+2)^{M}(K+1)(1)^{T}
. and
\nu=(s+2)(1)^{p+1}
.

This family has
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=2
.
We first apply the window
R_{1}
to
\lambda
and
\mu
, and then the second window
R_{2}
to
\lambda_{R_{1}}
and
\nu
, where the windows are given by

(46)

\mu_{R}=21

, and
\lambda_{R}=321

Corollary 3.25
.

If we assume the Window Lensing Conjecture (
3.8
), then there exists a rule
{\bm{g}}_{21,21}^{321}\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{321}(\mathbb{Z})
that simultaneously solves the 6 parameter family (
3.24
) of Jack LR coefficients using the composite window
R
in formula (
47
). That is,

(48)

{\bm{g}}_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\bm{F}_{R}\times({\bm{g}}_{21,21}^{321})_{R}.

So far in this article, we have discussed windowing as a way of reducing to the calculation of LR coefficients to simpler problems. Now, we reverse that previous perspective and view the windowed partitions as fixed, and consider varying the de-windowing via
R
. As we vary the 6 parameters
(K,M,N,T,p,s)
characterizing
R
, we can use other methods (such as directly computing the Jacks, or by using the recursive technique of
[
Sah10
]
) to compute the values of
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}(K,M,N,T,p,s)
. Then the system (
48
) gives an overdetermined system of linear equations for the rule
{\bm{g}}_{21,21}^{321}
, that is, for the integer coefficients
c_{\Phi}
within, according to the General Structure Conjecture (
2.6
). In particular, we looking for a rule of the form

(49)

{\bm{g}}_{21,21}^{321}=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{321}}c_{\Phi}\,\bm{G}_{\Phi}\,\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{321}(\mathbb{Z}),\quad\text{ with }c_{\Phi}\in\mathbb{Z}.

The set
\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{321}
has
2^{12}
elements, the channels
\Phi:={}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}};{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{I}}}jk\ell}\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{321}\cong\{U,L\}^{12}
. The corresponding channel factors (c.f (
8
))

(50)

\bm{G}_{\Phi}\equiv\bm{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}};{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{I}}}jk\ell}\coloneqq\frac{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$A$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern When such a rule is de-windowed and evaluated, the hook lengths of the symbols at
j,k,\ell
are indistinguishable, as they all either
1
or
\alpha
. Hence, we split off these three, and write the de-windowed rule over the
2^{9}
channels
\phi:={}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}};{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{I}}}}\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{21}\cong\{U,L\}^{9}
, as

(51)

[{\bm{g}}_{21,21}^{321}]_{R}=\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{21}}G_{\phi,R}\,\frac{d_{\phi}(\alpha)}{\alpha^{3}},\,\,\text{ with }d_{\phi}(\alpha)=\sum_{i=0}^{3}d_{\phi}^{i}\alpha^{i}\,\,\text{ and }d_{\phi}^{i}\in\mathbb{Z},

That is, only
G_{\phi,R}:=[\bm{G}_{\phi}]_{R}
depends on the de-windowed variables
(K,M,N,T,p,s)
, and the variables
d_{\phi}^{i}
track the
number
of
L
-hooks out of
j,k,\ell
, e.g.
d_{\phi}^{1}
counts choices where exactly two of
j,k,\ell
are upper hooks.

We also consider two operations on the set of channels. For
\phi={}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}};{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{I}}}}
, define

(52)

\phi^{T}\coloneqq{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}};{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{I}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}}},\qquad\bar{\phi}\coloneqq{}^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}};\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{G}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{H}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{I}}}}.

We can check computationally
7

3.5.
Redundancy

Although we claim to have found a solution (
3.26
) to the equation (
53
), each such solution comes with a large degree of redundancy. First, we propose a discrete
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
symmetry corresponding to channel conjugation
\Phi\to\bar{\Phi}
.

Conjecture 3.27
.

If a solution
\{d_{\phi}(\alpha)\}
is given to equation (
51
), then the set of channel solutions
\{d^{\prime}_{\phi}(\alpha)\}
given by

(55)

d^{\prime}_{\phi}(\alpha)\coloneqq\alpha^{3}d_{\bar{\phi}}(\alpha^{-1}),

is another solution. Here we have used the operation (
52
).

Next, we show there is a large kernel
K_{\phi}
solving the homogeneous equation

(56)

0=\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{21}}[\bm{G}_{\phi}]_{R}\,K_{\phi}.

Recall
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\sigma}(a)}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}+\beta
, where
\beta\coloneqq 1-\alpha
.

Definition 3.28
.

For any partition
\sigma
, and boxes
a,b,c\in\sigma
, define

(57)

k_{\sigma,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}\coloneqq{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}_{\sigma}(a)}-{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}_{\sigma}(b)}+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}_{\sigma}(c)}.

Note we have the symmetry
k_{\sigma,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}=k_{\sigma,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}}
. For the three boxes

a

b

c

of
\sigma=21
,
we define
k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}:=k_{21,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}
, which is

(58)

\begin{split}k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}=\alpha,\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}=-1,\,&\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}=1-2\alpha,\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}=\alpha,\\
k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}=-1,\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}=\alpha,\,&\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}=2-\alpha,\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}=-1.\end{split}

Note that we have the symmetry
-\alpha\,k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}(\alpha^{-1})=k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}(\alpha)
.

The following identity involving these
k
vectors be useful for describing the kernel.

Lemma 3.29
.

For any partition
\sigma
, and boxes
a,b,c\in\sigma
, we have

(59)

\sum_{A,B,C}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}_{\sigma}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}_{\sigma}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}_{\sigma}(c)}\times k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}=\left(-k_{\sigma,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}+k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}\right)\beta^{3}.

Proof.

We expand
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\sigma}(a)}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}+\beta
, to get

\displaystyle\sum_{A,B,C}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}_{\sigma}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}_{\sigma}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}_{\sigma}(c)}\times k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(c)}(\sum_{A,B,C}k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}})

\displaystyle+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}(\sum_{A,B}k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}})\beta

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(c)}(\sum_{A,C}k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}})\beta+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(c)}(\sum_{B,C}k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}})\beta

\displaystyle+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}(\sum_{A}k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}})\beta^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}(\sum_{B}k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}})\beta^{2}+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(c)}(\sum_{C}k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}})\beta^{2}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}\beta^{3}.

We then notice that

{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(a)}(\sum_{A}k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}})\beta^{2}+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(b)}(\sum_{B}k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}})\beta^{2}+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\sigma}(c)}(\sum_{C}k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}})\beta^{2}=-k_{\sigma,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}\beta^{3}

and all the othe sums vanish.

∎

We now express a large subspace of the kernel (
56
) in terms of the vector

given by (
58
).

where the unfilled, ungreyed boxes are assigned any fixed hook choices, and the greyed boxes are ignored.

Proof.

We only show this for the first and third sets of the 6 channel sets of (
61
), and the rest follow similarly. For the first, we need to show

(62)

\sum_{A,B,C}\left[\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\right]_{R}\times k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}=0.

We notice that (for
\mu=21
)

\left[\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\right]_{R}=k_{\mu,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}.

After de-windowing we find
{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\mu}(b)}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\mu}(a)}+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\mu}(c)}+1
, and hence
k_{\mu,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}=-1
.
Thus
-k_{\mu,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}+k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}=0
, and so using (
59
) we find that equation (
62
) holds.

Similarly, for the third channel set of (
61
), we need to show

(63)

\sum_{A,B,C}\frac{1}{\left[\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxGr}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxGr}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss${\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxGr}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}\right]_{R}}\times k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}=0.

We find that for
\lambda=321
(with ungreyed boxes
a,b,c\in\lambda
), this gives

{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}={h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(a)}+{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(c)}-\alpha,

and thus
k_{\lambda,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}=-1
.
We then write equation (
63
) as

\displaystyle\sum_{A,B,C}\frac{1}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}_{\lambda}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}_{\lambda}(c)}}\times k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{A,B,C}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{\bar{A}}}}_{\lambda}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{\bar{B}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{\bar{C}}}}_{\lambda}(c)}\times k^{\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}\bar{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(c)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(c)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\left(-k_{\lambda,abc}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}+k^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}\right)\beta^{3}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(a)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(c)}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(c)}}.

to see that (
60
) holds. The rest follow using the same technique.
∎

We now count the dimension of this subspace of the kernel. For each of the six sets of channels, we can make
2^{6}
choices on how to fill the remaining 6 ungreyed boxes. However, each of the
k
vectors (formula (
58
)) is a
linear
polynomial in
\alpha
, so at most we can multiply these kernel solutions by quadratic integer polynomials in
\alpha
(of dimension 3) to remain in the space of integer polynomials in
\alpha
of degree of most 3, where the
d_{\phi}(\alpha)
reside. Thus, this kernel subspace is
3\times 6\times 2^{6}
dimensional over
\mathbb{Z}
. On the other hand, there are
4\times 2^{9}=2^{11}
integer variables
d_{\phi}^{i}
.

Conjecture 3.31
.

The relations of Lemma (
3.30
) exhaust the kernel (
56
).

With this large kernel, we expect that there exists a more meaningful solution than the one given in (
3.26
), one whose coefficients
d_{\phi}
have a combinatorial interpretation. It is this path investigration that we propose might lead to insight into the Stanley conjecture. We will follow up on this in a future work.

4.
Factorization

In this section, we consider the situation where the window
R
splits the skew shape
\lambda/\mu
into two components
\lambda_{1}/\mu_{1}
, and
\lambda_{2}/\mu_{2}
. Bravi-Gandini
[
BG22
, Section 3.3]
, begin the investigation of how the skew Jack functions
J_{\lambda/\mu}
(and hence the Jack LR coefficient
g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
) factorize under such a splitting, which serves as the motivation for what is to come.

In the Schur case, the situation is quite simple.

Lemma 4.1
.

(64)

c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}c_{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}^{\lambda_{1}}c_{\mu_{2},\nu_{2}}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdot c_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}^{\nu}.

Proof.

Follows from the factorization of skew Schur functions
s_{\lambda/\mu}=s_{\lambda_{1}/\mu_{2}}s_{\lambda_{2}/\mu_{2}}
.
∎

Bravi-Gandini give an extension of this result to skew Jack functions in a special case.

Theorem 4.2

( Basic Factorization,
[
BG22
, Proposition 21]
)
.

Let
\mu
be a rectangular partition, with
\mu\subset\lambda
. Suppose
\lambda/\mu
has two connected components, whose shapes are those of partitions
\lambda_{1}
and
\lambda_{2}
. Suppose
\nu
satisifes
c_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=1
and
c_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\nu}=1
, then we have
8
8

8

We have left in the two factors of
g_{\emptyset,\lambda_{i}}^{\lambda_{i}}=1
to express similarity to equation (
64
)

(65)

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=\prod_{b\in\mu}\frac{h^{\bullet}_{\mu}(b)}{h^{\bullet}_{\lambda}(b)}\times g_{\emptyset,\lambda_{1}}^{\lambda_{1}}\,g_{\emptyset,\lambda_{2}}^{\lambda_{2}}\cdot f^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}_{\nu},

where we have defined

(66)

f^{\mu,\nu}_{\lambda}\coloneqq g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}\frac{j_{\lambda}}{j_{\mu}j_{\nu}},

and where the fraction of hooks in (
65
) has the following property: upper (and hence lower) hooks appear the same number of times in the numerator and denominator - a property we call
balanced
.

Example 4.3
.

g_{2,211}^{321}=\frac{\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 4.1.
Conjectures

In this section, we assume the earlier conjectures regarding windowing and we speculate about a window-compatible generalization of the basic factorization result (
4.2
). One might expect such a generalization to take the form:

(67)

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=F_{R}\times\sum_{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}\left[\prod_{b\in\mu_{R}}\frac{\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\mu_{R}}(b)}{\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\lambda_{R}}(b)}\right]_{R}\times g_{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}^{\lambda_{1}}g_{\mu_{2},\nu_{2}}^{\lambda_{2}}f_{\nu}^{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}},

mimicing the form of (
64
) and (
65
). We conjecture that a formula similar to this holds.

Conjecture 4.5

( General Factorization )
.

Let
R
be a window (potentially composite).
Let
\kappa
be a
factorization region
, that is, a partition satisfying
\kappa\subseteq\mu_{R}\subset\lambda_{R}
, with the property that
\lambda_{R}/\kappa
has multiple components
\{\lambda_{i}\}
, and similarly let
\{\mu_{j}\}
be the components of
\mu_{R}/\kappa
(if they exist). We conjecture that the Jack LR coefficient can be expanded in the following form

(68)

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=F_{R}\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}}G_{\Phi,R}^{\kappa}\times H_{\Phi}(\vec{v}),

where,

•

\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{\kappa,\emptyset}^{\kappa}
are a set of
factorization
channels that are independent of the window
R
.

•

To each channel
\Phi
is associated a
channel factor

\bm{G}^{\kappa}_{\Phi}
(compare to (
8
)), which is the product of hooks for each box
b\in\kappa
inside both
\lambda_{R}
and
\mu_{R}
.

(69)

\bm{G}^{\kappa}_{\Phi}=\prod_{b\in\kappa}\frac{{\bm{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{{\mu_{R}}}(b)}}{{\bm{h}^{\Phi_{b}}_{{\lambda_{R}}}(b)}}.

As before, we let
G_{\Phi,R}^{\kappa}\coloneqq[\bm{G}^{\kappa}_{\Phi}]_{R}
.

•

The
channel solutions

H_{\Phi}(\vec{v})\coloneqq[\bm{H}_{\Phi}]_{R}
are rational functions in
\alpha
that are ‘local’ to
\lambda_{i},\mu_{j},\nu_{R}
. That is, that depend only on the vector of
local parameters

\vec{v}
characterising the de-windowed shapes of
\lambda_{i},\mu_{j}
and
\nu_{R}
. Typically, these parameters are of the form

v=[\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}}_{\sigma}(b)]_{R}

for
\sigma=\lambda_{R}
,
\mu_{R}
or
\nu_{R}
and
b\in\sigma/\kappa
, or

(70)

v=[\bm{e}_{\kappa}(b)]_{R}\coloneqq\left[\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\lambda_{R}}(b)-\bm{h}^{\bullet}_{\mu_{R}}(b)\right]_{R}

for
b\in\kappa
.

In short, the window factor
F_{R}
and the channel factors
G_{\Phi,R}^{\kappa}
depend on all the parameters of the window
R
, but the channel solutions depend on only a subset of these parameters.

One is tempted to think of the channel solutions as capturing the factorized product
H_{\Phi}\cong g_{\mu_{1},\nu_{1}}^{\lambda_{1}}g_{\mu_{2},\nu_{2}}^{\lambda_{2}}f_{\nu}^{\nu_{1},\nu_{2}}
, for
\Phi
corresponding to the
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}
splitting, but we find that such an interpretation does not hold in examples with
c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}>1
.

The claims of the above conjecture are best illustrated with examples.

Example 4.6
.

The Bravi-Gandini factorization result (
4.2
) reduces to the case where
\mu
is a rectangular partition and
\kappa=\mu
(hence
\mu_{i}=\emptyset
). In this case, the Jack LR coefficient factors over a single channel
\Phi
, with
H_{\Phi}=f^{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}_{\nu}
. In the example (
4.4
), we use the window

(71)

R=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxS}\vbox to11.65326pt{\vss\hbox to11.24445pt{\hss$$\hss}\vss}}\kern-11.65326pt\vrule height=11.65326pt,width=0.40881pt\kern which gives
\mu_{R}=2^{2}=\kappa
. We sum over the single channel
\Phi:={}^{\left[{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}\right]}_{\left[{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}\right]}
, with

(72)

g^{43221}_{222,2211}=F_{R}\times G_{\Phi,R}^{\kappa}\times g_{\emptyset,21}^{21}\,g_{\emptyset,21}^{21}\,f^{21,21}_{2211}.

In this case the general factorization conjecture (
4.5
) correctly predicts that the channel coefficient
H_{\Phi}=f_{21,21}^{2211}=f_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}^{\nu}
is only a function of the the shapes
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
and
\nu
.

.

3.15

In the first factored windowed region
\lambda_{1}
, we have the solution to the Maximal Filling (MF) problem
g_{\emptyset,11}^{11}
, and in the second region
\lambda_{2}
we have the solution to the MF problem
g_{3311,432}^{44432}
. This corresponds to splitting
\nu
as the horizontal sum
\nu=542=432+11
. We find the example factorizes over
\kappa
with a single channel
\Phi={}^{[{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}]^{\prime}}_{[{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}]^{\prime}}
(as read off from figure (
30
)), where the following holds

(73)

g_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=F_{R}\times G_{\Phi,R}^{\kappa}\times H_{\Phi},

where the channel solution splits accordingly

(74)

H_{\Phi}=g_{3311,432}^{44432}\,g_{\emptyset,11}^{11}\,f^{432,11}_{542}.

The factorization conjecture (
4.5
) claims that

(76)

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=F_{R}\times\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}}G_{\Phi,R}\times H_{\Phi}(p,s)

That is, the channel solutions
H_{\Phi}
only depend on two parameters
(p,s)
that characterize the shapes of the de-windowing of
\mu_{R}/\kappa
,
\lambda_{R}/\kappa
and
\nu_{R}
, whereas the window factor
F_{R}
and the channel solutions
G_{\Phi,R}
depend on all six parameters.

To set notation, for the factorization region
\kappa=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$x$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$y$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$z$\hss}\vss}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
, let the channel
\Phi={}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}
correspond to the channel factor

\bm{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}:=\frac{\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}}_{\mu_{R}}(x)\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}}_{\mu_{R}}(y)\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mu_{R}}(z)}{\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{D}}}}_{\lambda_{R}}(x)\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}}_{\lambda_{R}}(y)\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\lambda_{R}}(z)}.

For
\nu_{R}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxS}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$a$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxS}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$b$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxS}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$c$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
,
Let
\bm{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}_{\nu_{R}}=-\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{A}}}_{\nu_{R}}(a)+\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}_{\nu_{R}}(b)-\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}_{\nu_{R}}(c)
.

Conjecture 4.8
.

A solution to equation (
76
) is given as follows.
Define the expression

{\bf{Z}}=\frac{\bm{j}_{\nu_{R}}}{\bm{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\nu_{R}}\bm{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\nu_{R}}\prod_{b\in\kappa}\bm{e}_{\kappa}(b)},

where we have used the notation (
70
),
with de-windowed evaluation

Z(p,s):=\left[\bf{Z}\right]_{R}=\frac{({{\lfloor}\!{p,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{p+1,\!0}\!{\rfloor}\!})({{\lfloor}\!{1,\!s}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{0,\!s+1}\!{\rfloor}\!})({{\lfloor}\!{p+2,\!s+1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{p+1,\!s+2}\!{\rfloor}\!})}{{{\lfloor}\!{0,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!0}\!{\rfloor}\!}\cdot{{\lfloor}\!{p+1,\!1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{1,\!s+1}\!{\rfloor}\!}{{\lfloor}\!{p+1,\!s+1}\!{\rfloor}\!}}.

The channel solutions
H_{\Phi}(p,s)\coloneqq[\bm{H}_{\Phi}]_{R}
defined below over the 8 channels solve the equation (
76
),

\Phi

\bm{H}_{\Phi}/\bm{Z}
