\frac{1}{\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\nu_{R}}(a)}-\frac{1}{\bm{\beta}}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}

\frac{1}{\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\nu_{R}}(c)}+\frac{1}{\bm{\beta}}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}

\frac{1}{\bm{h}^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\nu_{R}}(c)}-\frac{1}{\bm{\beta}}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}

\frac{1}{\bm{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\nu_{R}}}-\frac{1}{\bm{\beta}}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}

\frac{1}{\bm{k}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\nu_{R}}}+\frac{1}{\bm{\beta}}

where
\bm{\beta}=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxL}\vbox to10.63098pt{\vss\hbox to10.22217pt{\hss$L$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.63098pt\vrule height=10.63098pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 10.22217pt\vrule height=10.63098pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-10.63098pt\hrule width=11.0398pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 10.22217pt\hrule width=11.0398pt,height=0.40881pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}-\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.40881pt\vbox{\kern 0.2044pt\hbox{\kern 0.40881pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxU}\vbox to10.63098pt{\vss\hbox to10.22217pt{\hss$U$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.63098pt\vrule height=10.63098pt,width=0.40881pt\kern 10.22217pt\vrule height=10.63098pt,width=0.40881pt}\kern-0.2044pt\kern-10.63098pt\hrule width=11.0398pt,height=0.40881pt\kern 10.22217pt\hrule width=11.0398pt,height=0.40881pt}\crcr}}\kern 345.4pt}}$}
satisfies
[\bm{\beta}]_{R}=\beta
.
Note that this solution is only over channels of the form
G^{X}_{X}
.
Furthermore this solution has the two symmetries

(77)

\bm{H}_{\Phi^{T}}=(\bm{H}_{\Phi})^{T},\qquad\bm{H}_{\bar{\Phi}}=\overline{\bm{H}_{\Phi}},

where
\bar{\bm{\beta}}=-\bm{\beta}
and
\bm{\beta}^{T}=\bm{\beta}
, and
\bm{Z}
is invariant under both. This the unique solution with 8 or less non-vanishing channel solutions.

Example 4.9
.

We demonstrate the decomposition of

g_{21,21}^{321}=\frac{6\alpha(2+11\alpha+2\alpha^{2})}{(2+\alpha)(1+2\alpha)(3+2\alpha)(2+3\alpha)}

as
g_{21,21}^{321}=\sum_{\Phi}G_{\Phi}\times H_{\Phi}
, with

\Phi

G_{\Phi}\times H_{\Phi}

\Phi

G_{\Phi}\times H_{\Phi}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}

\frac{-3\alpha^{4}(2+\alpha)}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(1+2\alpha)(2+3\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}

\frac{3\alpha(1+2\alpha)}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(2+\alpha)(3+2\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}

\frac{\alpha(-1+2\alpha)(1+2\alpha)}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(2+3\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}

\frac{\alpha(-2+\alpha)(2+\alpha)}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(3+2\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}

\frac{\alpha(-1+2\alpha)(1+2\alpha)}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(2+3\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}

\frac{\alpha(-2+\alpha)(2+\alpha)}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(3+2\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}

\frac{\alpha(-2+\alpha)(1+2\alpha)^{2}}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(2+\alpha)(2+3\alpha)}

{}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}

\frac{\alpha^{2}(-1+2\alpha)(2+\alpha)^{2}}{(\alpha-1)(1+\alpha)^{3}(1+2\alpha)(3+2\alpha)}

4.2.
Channel Identities

Note that in the formula (
4.8
), each of the channel solutions
H_{\Phi}
has a pole as
\alpha\to 1
, i.e. as
\beta\to 0
. However, the combined residue at this pole vanishes, as shown by the following result.

Lemma 4.10
.

\sum_{X\in\{U,L\}^{3}}(-1)^{\#U(X)}G^{X}_{X}=-\beta^{3}\frac{\prod_{b\in\kappa}e_{\kappa}(b)}{j_{\lambda,\kappa}}

where
\#U(X)
counts the number of upper hooks
(U)
in
X
, and
e_{\kappa}(b):=[\bm{e}_{\kappa}(b)]_{R}
as in (
70
).

Proof.

(78)

\displaystyle\sum_{X\in\{U,L\}^{3}}(-1)^{\#U(X)}G^{X}_{X}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{b\in\kappa}\left(\tfrac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}-\tfrac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\right)

(79)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{b\in\kappa}\left(\tfrac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)-h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}-\tfrac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\mu}(b)-h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\right)

(80)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{b\in\kappa}(h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)-h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b))\left(\tfrac{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)-h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}{h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b)h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}_{\lambda}(b)}\right)

(81)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{3}\tfrac{\prod_{b\in\kappa}(h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\mu}(b)-h^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}_{\lambda}(b))}{j_{\lambda,\kappa}}.

∎

The following results show that we always have redundancies between factorization channels, that is, there always exists linear relations between channel factors for
\kappa
with coefficients that are valued in the local parameters, e.g.
e_{\kappa}(b)
.

Lemma 4.11
.

For a box
b\in\kappa
, define the single channel factor

G^{U}_{L}:=\frac{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\mu}(b)}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}.

The following linear relation holds between channel factors

(82)

G^{L}_{L}-G^{L}_{U}-G^{U}_{L}+G^{U}_{U}=\frac{-\beta}{e_{\kappa}(b)}\cdot(G^{L}_{L}-G^{U}_{U}).

Proof.

We start with

G^{U}_{U}-G^{L}_{U}=\frac{-\beta}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}

So

\displaystyle\frac{1}{-\beta}(G^{U}_{U}-G^{L}_{U}-G^{U}_{L}+G^{L}_{L})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}-\frac{1}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e_{\kappa}(b)}\left(\frac{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}-{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\mu}(b)}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}-\frac{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}-{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}(b)}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{e_{\kappa}(b)}\left(\frac{-{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\mu}(b)}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}-\frac{-{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}(b)}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}(b)}}\right).

∎

We can easily generalize Lemma (
4.11
) to any
\kappa
.

Corollary 4.12
.

(83)

\sum_{X,Y\in\{U,L\}^{|\kappa|}}(-1)^{\#U(X)+\#U(Y)}G^{X}_{Y}=\frac{(-\beta)^{|\kappa|}}{\prod_{b\in\kappa}{e_{\kappa}(b)}}\sum_{X\in\{U,L\}^{|\kappa|}}(-1)^{\#U(X)}G^{X}_{X}.

4.3.
Regularity

The individual channel solutions
H_{\Phi}
in Conjecture (
4.8
) have poles in the Schur limit,
\alpha\to 1
. This is likely a consequence of the highly factorized nature of that particular solution.

Corresponding to each factorization channel, we define

(84)

\tilde{C}_{\Phi}(\vec{v})\coloneqq\frac{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\lambda}}{}}{{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\mu}}{}{h^{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}_{\nu}}{}}\times F_{R}\times G_{\Phi}\times H_{\Phi}(\vec{v}).

If the limit
\left(\tilde{C}_{\Phi}(\vec{v})\right)_{\alpha\to 1}
exists for all
\Phi\in\Gamma
and
\vec{v}\in\mathcal{T}
, then we say the channel solutions
\{H_{\Phi}\}
are
regular
.
In that case we can associate a
local channel coefficient
,

(85)

C_{\Phi}(\vec{v})\coloneqq\left(\tilde{C}_{\Phi}(\vec{v})\right)_{\alpha\to 1}.

Note, one must take the
\alpha
limit
after
assigning values to
\vec{v}
. We will often see factors such as
\frac{(-1+\alpha)}{(t-1+\alpha)}
, where the order of these limits matters when
t=0
.

In the case of a regular factorization, we have the following decomposition of Schur LR coefficients (c.f. equation (
2
))

c_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}=\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}}C_{\Phi}(\vec{v}).

Conjecture 4.13
.

For certain factorizations
\kappa
, there exists factorization solutions such that
C_{\Phi}(\vec{v})\in\mathbb{Z}
, and are independent of
\vec{v}
.

Regular Solution

\kappa=21
, here we instead factorize over the smaller
\kappa=(2)
.

With this, the channel solutions will be independent of

.

.

) claims there exists a solution of the form

g_{\mu,\nu}^{\lambda}=F_{R}\times\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa}}G_{\Phi,R}\times H_{\Phi}(p,s,T,K)

That is, the channel factors

only depend on the parameters

that characterize the shapes of the de-windowing of

,

and
\nu_{R}
.

For the factorization region
\kappa=\hbox{$\vbox{\hbox{\vtop{\halign{&\opttoksa@YT={\font@YT}\getcolor@YT{\save@YT{\opttoksb@YT}}\nil@YT\getcolor@YT{\startbox@@YT\the\opttoksa@YT\the\opttoksb@YT}#\endbox@YT\cr\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxS}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$y$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}&\lower 0.39993pt\vbox{\kern 0.19997pt\hbox{\kern 0.39993pt\hbox{\pagecolor{boxS}\vbox to10.39995pt{\vss\hbox to10.00002pt{\hss$z$\hss}\vss}}\kern-10.39995pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\vrule height=10.39995pt,width=0.39993pt}\kern-0.19997pt\kern-10.39995pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt\kern 10.00002pt\hrule width=10.79988pt,height=0.39993pt}\crcr}}\kern 690.4pt}}$}
, let the channel
\Phi=\frac{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}
correspond to the channel solution

\bm{G}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}:=\frac{\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{B}}}}_{\mu_{R}}(y)\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{C}}}}_{\mu_{R}}(z)}{\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}}}_{\lambda_{R}}(y)\bm{h}^{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{F}}}}_{\lambda_{R}}(z)}.

Conjecture 4.14
.

The following
9
9

We shift the variables here for simplicity of the formula

regular
channel solutions
H_{\Phi}
over the four channels

), with

1

This solution has redundancies, as was noted before in equation (
82
). There is also a two parameter set of redundancies between the 6
balanced
channels for
\kappa
, that is, channels with the same number of upper hooks in the numerator as denominator. Indeed, there exists solutions over just
three
balanced channels, however these solutions are irregular. The solution (
4.14
) is preferable as it is over the fewest channels (i.e, four) while consisting of regular channel solutions.

4.5.
Examples

We conclude with several important cases of this 6-parameter family.

Example 4.15

(Return to P. Hanlon’s example
[
Sta89
, p114]
)
.

Take
\mu=31
,
\nu=21
,
\lambda=421
.
It is known that

g_{31,21}^{421}=\frac{\alpha(9+97\alpha+294\alpha^{2}+321\alpha^{3}+131\alpha^{4}+12\alpha^{5})}{3(1+\alpha)^{3}(2+\alpha)(1+2\alpha)^{2}(1+3\alpha)}.

This is the
N=1,M=0,t=0,p=0,s=0
case of the windowing
R
. The solution (
4.14
) gives the following expression for this LR coefficient

\displaystyle g_{31,21}^{421}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{R}\times\sum_{\Phi}G_{\Phi}\times H_{\Phi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1+\alpha)}\times

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(1+3\alpha)(2\alpha)}{(2+4\alpha)(1+3\alpha)}\times\frac{(1-\alpha)(1+3\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)^{2}(1+2\alpha)}\right.

\displaystyle+\,\,\frac{(1+3\alpha)(1+\alpha)}{(3+3\alpha)(1+3\alpha)}\times\frac{(1-\alpha)(-\alpha)(3+\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)^{2}(2+\alpha)}

\displaystyle+\,\,\frac{(1+3\alpha)(1+\alpha)}{(2+4\alpha)(2+2\alpha)}\times\frac{2(2\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)^{2}}

\displaystyle+\,\left.\frac{(2+2\alpha)(2\alpha)}{(3+3\alpha)(1+3\alpha)}\times\frac{2(2\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)^{2}}\right).

This is corresponds to the windowing
R
with
t=a_{1}-a_{2}-1
,
p=0
,
s=0
,
N=0
,
M=a_{2}-1
. Using the solution of Conjecture (
4.14
)

g_{(a_{1},a_{2}),21}^{(a_{1}+1,a_{2}+1,1)}=F_{R}\times\sum_{\Phi}G_{\Phi,R}\times H_{\Phi}(p,s,t).

we find the LR coefficient is given be the formula

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(t+2\alpha)}{(t-1+\alpha)}\frac{(\alpha)^{2}}{(a_{1}-1+2\alpha)(a_{2}-1+\alpha)}\times

\displaystyle\left(\frac{(a_{1}-1+2\alpha)(a_{2}-1+\alpha)}{(a_{1}+3\alpha)(a_{2}+2\alpha)}\times\frac{2(t)(1-\alpha)(t+3\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)(t+2\alpha)(t+1+2\alpha)}\right.

\displaystyle+\,\,\frac{(a_{1}-1+2\alpha)(a_{2})}{(a_{1}+1+2\alpha)(a_{2}+2\alpha)}\times\frac{-2(1-\alpha)\alpha(1+2\alpha)(t+2+\alpha)}{(t+\alpha)(t+1+\alpha)(t+2\alpha)(t+1+2\alpha)}

\displaystyle+\,\,\frac{(a_{1}-1+2\alpha)(a_{2})}{(a_{1}+3\alpha)(a_{2}+1+1\alpha)}\times\frac{2(t)(1+2\alpha)(t-1+2\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)(t+\alpha)(t+2\alpha)}

\displaystyle+\,\,\left.\frac{(a_{1}+\alpha)(a_{2}-1+\alpha)}{(a_{1}+1+2\alpha)(a_{2}+2\alpha)}\times\frac{2(t)(1+2\alpha)(t-1+2\alpha)}{(1+\alpha)(t+\alpha)(t+2\alpha)}\right).

This factorization results in the Schur LR sum

(89)

c_{a_{1}a_{2},21}^{(a_{1}+1,a_{2}+1,1)}:=C_{\frac{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}}+C_{\frac{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}}+C_{\frac{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}}}+C_{\frac{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}{{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{L}}}{\scriptscriptstyle{\mathrm{U}}}}}=\begin{cases}0+0+1+1&a_{1}>a_{2}\\
0+1+0+0&a_{1}=a_{2}.\end{cases}

Here we see the regularity of the solutions continues formally as
a_{1}\to a_{2}
(i.e.
t\to-1
and the window
R
requires modification), and there is a discrete discontinuity in the channel factors
C_{\Phi}
at this ‘boundary’ of the parameter space where the problem degenerates to
c=1
.

We find for
a_{1}=13,a_{2}=5
, this gives

(90)

g_{(13,5),(2,1)}^{(14,6,1)}=\frac{3\alpha^{2}(772+335\alpha+13\alpha^{2})}{(6+\alpha)(7+\alpha)(9+\alpha)(5+2\alpha)(13+3\alpha)}.

5.
Conclusions

We have formulated the Window Lensing Conjecture (
3.8
) and presented evidence for its validity. This conjecture may help improve the understanding of Jack Littlewood-Richardson coefficients, and can hopefully lay the groundwork for an eventual proof of the Stanley conjectures. This Window Lensing property is essentially the minimal property that covers both the Maximal Filling case (which we’ve proved it holds for) and the Rectangular Union case (for which it holds by inspection). However, beyond those
c=1
examples, we have presented three formulas for the
c=2
family with windowed rule
{\bm{g}}_{21,21}^{321}
, each of which displays particular curious properties.

Firstly, Formula (
3.26
) gives a conjectural 20-term solution
\{d_{\phi}(\alpha)\}
in the most precise form, demonstrating the validity of the General Structure conjecture. This example exhibits a single symmetry, and has a large degree of redundancy through the kernel (
3.30
).

Secondly, Formula (
4.8
) on the factorized set of 8 channels
\{H_{\Phi}(p,s)\}
, gives our most symmetric form of an answer. This formula is the most combinatorical in nature, with the Channel Solutions
H_{\Phi}
being given explicitly in terms of hooks and the two symmetries (
77
) are manifest. This formula is has no redundancies within the 8 symmetric channels, but each channel solution is singular in the
\alpha\to 1
limit.

Lastly, Formula (
4.14
) over the factorized 4 channels
\{H_{\Phi}(p,s,t)\}
, represents our most economical formula. This solution is regular, and can easily be used to compute Jack LR coefficients in some large examples.

These three solutions present the same windowed content, as expressed in the following equalities

\displaystyle\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{H}_{21,21}^{21}}G_{\phi,R}\,\frac{d_{\phi}(\alpha)}{\alpha^{3}}.

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa=21}}G_{\Phi,R}\times H_{\Phi}(p,s)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\Phi\in\mathcal{H}_{\kappa=2}}G_{\Phi,R}\times H_{\Phi}(p,s,t).

The work continues in the hope for a
combinatorical
description of any one of these three solutions.

6.
